I’ll sit down and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, because there’s this project I’m trying to get off the ground, and then I’m pitching it to community leaders, and then I’m back rewriting the backend at 2 a.m. because the whole architecture needs to support the installations properly.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like I’m not answering the question, but it’s connected—I don’t just fidget with my hands, I start mock-planning whole exhibits and messaging other developers to help me, all hours, because it feels… bigger than my regular job now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing causing problems in your personal relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems. Work, especially. They put me on leave after I, um, pushed changes without approval. I thought it needed to go out right then, like the process was just slowing down the whole thing, but apparently bypassing that was a big deal.\n\nAnd at home… my wife says things feel unstable. She keeps bringing up the spending and the arguments, and I try to explain that some of the purchases were for the project, not just random, but then we end up fighting about that too. So, yeah, it’s affecting daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're not working on your projects or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yeah—overwhelmed, or maybe… agitated is closer? If I’m not working on it, it feels like I’m missing the moment, like there’s this current running through me and I’m supposed to use it.\n\nI’ll wake up already planning features, like the interface flow, and then it turns into exhibition layouts, and then I’m thinking about how the press release should frame it—because wording matters, you know, people ignore things if the angle is wrong—and then I’m back to code changes before I’ve even had coffee. Sitting still just feels like wasting time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself taking on more projects or tasks than you can realistically handle?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, “too much” is kind of relative, right? Because if people understood the scope of it, it wouldn’t seem like too much. But I guess from the outside, yes.\n\nI’ve been emailing curators, trying to pitch community leaders, rewriting parts of the backend, sketching out installation ideas—well, not sketching exactly, more like mock-planning how the spaces should work—and then I’m also reaching out to other developers at weird hours to see if they can help. It’s not just one task anymore, it’s like all these connected pieces.\n\nAnd I know I still have my regular job—or had, I mean, that’s complicated—but this project just feels bigger than that right now, so I keep adding things because they all feel necessary.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean… I thought I was thinking it through at the time. It didn’t feel impulsive, it felt like timing. Like, if you wait too long, people move on, the whole window closes.

And I pushed an update without getting approval first. Not because I was trying to be reckless, it just felt like another review cycle would kill the opportunity. Like everything would stall out right when it needed to move.

I mean, there is a clear goal. It’s not just the idea buzzing in my head, you know? I’m trying to make the project real—like actually get it in front of people. It’s not just the idea buzzing in my head, you know? I’m trying to make the project real—like actually get it in front of people. That’s why I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out how the installation would work, trying to pull in other developers.\n\nBut… it’s hard to separate that from not being able to let it go. Because once I see the path, I keep thinking, okay, this person needs to hear about it, this part needs to be rebuilt, this could connect to that group. And then suddenly it’s two in the morning and I’m still sending messages.\n\nMy regular job just feels kind of… narrow compared to it right now. This feels bigger, like it matters more. So yes, I have a goal, but the idea also sort of grabs me and keeps pulling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're drawn to new ideas or projects because they're exciting or thrilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exciting is part of it, I guess. It’s not like I’m chasing a thrill exactly, but when the project starts connecting in my head, it feels alive. Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to miss it.\n\nSo I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out installation plans—well, mock-planning them, I don’t have the space yet—and trying to get other developers interested. And it happens at weird hours because that’s when the pieces line up for me.\n\nIt feels bigger than my regular job right now. Not just more fun, but more important somehow. Like the regular work is just tickets and meetings, and this has… I don’t know, reach.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your behavior is causing you a lot of stress or distress?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / software_developer / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d call it distress exactly. Stress, maybe, but it’s hard to separate from just having a lot happening.

Like, when I’m in it, it feels productive, not stressful—more like I’m finally following the thread. But then people around me react like I’m creating a storm, and then I start feeling stressed about their reactions, if that makes sense.

And, sorry, I’m probably going sideways here, but it reminds me of when you’re debugging something and everyone’s saying “stop touching it,” but you can see the fix is right there. So… yeah, maybe some stress. I don’t know how much is the behavior itself versus everyone trying to slow me down.

Yeah… strained, definitely. I don’t know if I’d say “lost friendships,” exactly, but things at home are not good.

I want to say it’s a clear plan, because in my head it is. Like, there’s a sequence — hardware, outreach, visuals, deployment — it all connects.

So I don’t know. It felt planned to me, but I guess I was acting before anyone else had really agreed to the plan.

My wife keeps saying the spending and the arguments are making things feel unstable. I think she means not just money, but the whole atmosphere, like she doesn’t know what I’m going to decide next. It feels like the project is bigger than my regular job now, and I keep working on it at all hours because stopping feels like losing the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas because you have a clear plan in mind or because you're acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: I want to say it’s a clear plan, because in my head it is. Like, there’s a sequence — hardware, outreach, visuals, deployment — it all connects.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, some of it probably looked impulsive from the outside. I spent money we hadn’t agreed on, on GPUs and print materials and these promo videos, because I felt like if I waited, the moment would pass. And at work, I pushed an update without approval because another review cycle felt like… like letting the whole opportunity die on the vine.\n\nSo I don’t know. It felt planned to me, but I guess I was acting before anyone else had really agreed to the plan.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you're drawn to these new projects because they offer a sense of novelty?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 43 / software_developer / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Manic_Distractibility\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I can't keep my mind on one thing anymore—I'll be fixing a rendering bug and a Slack ping, a color pattern, or even a siren outside pulls me into a whole new idea before I finish the line of code.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, novelty is part of it, but it’s more like everything grabs me before I’m done with the last thing.

Like I’ll be fixing a rendering bug, and then a Slack ping comes in and suddenly I’m thinking about how that message connects to the launch plan, or I’ll notice a color pattern in something and it turns into a whole visual direction. Even a siren outside the window can pull me into some new idea before I’ve finished the line of code I was writing.

That’s actually part of why I came in. I can’t really keep my mind on one thing anymore. It’s not just “oh, this is exciting,” it’s like my attention keeps getting yanked sideways.

It’s… it’s making it hard to get anything done.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah, restless, sure, but it’s more like I can’t stop doing things. I’ll sit down and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, because there’s this project I’m trying to get off the ground, and then I’m pitching it to community leaders, and then I’m back rewriting the backend at 2 a.m. because the whole architecture needs to support the installations properly.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like I’m not answering the question, but it’s connected—I don’t just fidget with my hands, I start mock-planning whole exhibits and messaging other developers to help me, all hours, because it feels… bigger than my regular job now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing causing problems in your personal relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems. Work, especially. They put me on leave after I, um, pushed changes without approval. I thought it needed to go out right then, like the process was just slowing down the whole thing, but apparently bypassing that was a big deal.\n\nAnd at home… my wife says things feel unstable. She keeps bringing up the spending and the arguments, and I try to explain that some of the purchases were for the project, not just random, but then we end up fighting about that too. So, yeah, it’s affecting daily life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're not working on your projects or ideas?\n\nPatient: Yeah—overwhelmed, or maybe… agitated is closer? If I’m not working on it, it feels like I’m missing the moment, like there’s this current running through me and I’m supposed to use it.\n\nI’ll wake up already planning features, like the interface flow, and then it turns into exhibition layouts, and then I’m thinking about how the press release should frame it—because wording matters, you know, people ignore things if the angle is wrong—and then I’m back to code changes before I’ve even had coffee. Sitting still just feels like wasting time.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself taking on more projects or tasks than you can realistically handle?\n\nPatient: Yeah, probably. I mean, “too much” is kind of relative, right? Because if people understood the scope of it, it wouldn’t seem like too much. But I guess from the outside, yes.\n\nI’ve been emailing curators, trying to pitch community leaders, rewriting parts of the backend, sketching out installation ideas—well, not sketching exactly, more like mock-planning how the spaces should work—and then I’m also reaching out to other developers at weird hours to see if they can help. It’s not just one task anymore, it’s like all these connected pieces.\n\nAnd I know I still have my regular job—or had, I mean, that’s complicated—but this project just feels bigger than that right now, so I keep adding things because they all feel necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things because you genuinely believe they need to be done, or because you just can't help doing them?\n\nPatient: I think… both, maybe? I genuinely believe it needs to be done. Like, I’m not just clicking around because I’m bored. The curator emails, the community leader pitches, rewriting the backend, planning how the installation would work, trying to get other developers involved—even if it’s late—it all feels connected.\n\nBut then, yeah, sometimes it also feels like I can’t not do it. Like if I pause, the whole thing loses momentum. And my regular job feels… smaller than this right now, honestly. This project feels bigger, like it has to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I mean… I thought I was thinking it through at the time. It didn’t feel impulsive, it felt like timing. Like, if you wait too long, people move on, the whole window closes.\n\nBut yeah, looking back, maybe I skipped steps. I spent money we hadn’t really agreed on—GPUs, some print materials, a promo video thing, which I still think could’ve been useful because people don’t read long proposals anymore, they need to see it—but my partner was upset because we hadn’t talked it through.\n\nAnd I pushed an update without getting approval first. Not because I was trying to be reckless, it just felt like another review cycle would kill the opportunity. Like everything would stall out right when it needed to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior has led to any significant problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like everything I was doing was just wrong, and I don’t think it was. But my employer put me on leave after I pushed those changes without going through approval. They said I bypassed the process and it created problems.\n\nAnd at home it’s been… tense. My wife keeps saying the spending and the arguments are making things feel unstable. I think she means not just money, but the whole atmosphere, like she doesn’t know what I’m going to decide next. I don’t want it to be that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas because you have a clear goal in mind or because you just can't resist the idea itself?\n\nPatient: I mean, there is a clear goal. It’s not just the idea buzzing in my head, you know? I’m trying to make the project real—like actually get it in front of people. That’s why I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out how the installation would work, trying to pull in other developers.\n\nBut… it’s hard to separate that from not being able to let it go. Because once I see the path, I keep thinking, okay, this person needs to hear about it, this part needs to be rebuilt, this could connect to that group. And then suddenly it’s two in the morning and I’m still sending messages.\n\nMy regular job just feels kind of… narrow compared to it right now. This feels bigger, like it matters more. So yes, I have a goal, but the idea also sort of grabs me and keeps pulling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're drawn to new ideas or projects because they're exciting or thrilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exciting is part of it, I guess. It’s not like I’m chasing a thrill exactly, but when the project starts connecting in my head, it feels alive. Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to miss it.\n\nSo I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out installation plans—well, mock-planning them, I don’t have the space yet—and trying to get other developers interested. And it happens at weird hours because that’s when the pieces line up for me.\n\nIt feels bigger than my regular job right now. Not just more fun, but more important somehow. Like the regular work is just tickets and meetings, and this has… I don’t know, reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing you a lot of stress or distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it distress exactly. Stress, maybe, but it’s hard to separate from just having a lot happening.\n\nLike, when I’m in it, it feels productive, not stressful—more like I’m finally following the thread. But then people around me react like I’m creating a storm, and then I start feeling stressed about their reactions, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd, sorry, I’m probably going sideways here, but it reminds me of when you’re debugging something and everyone’s saying “stop touching it,” but you can see the fix is right there. So… yeah, maybe some stress. I don’t know how much is the behavior itself versus everyone trying to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friendships or strained relationships with family members due to your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… strained, definitely. I don’t know if I’d say “lost friendships,” exactly, but things at home are not good.\n\nMy wife says the spending and the arguments have made everything feel unstable, like she doesn’t know what I’m going to do next. And I get defensive because in my head I’m thinking, well, you have to invest in something if you want it to work, right? Like you can’t build a real thing with no tools, no outreach, no—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, it’s strained things. And work too, actually. My employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes myself. I thought I was fixing something, moving it forward, but they didn’t see it that way. So… yeah, relationships and trust, both at home and at work, have taken a hit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're often looking for the next new or exciting thing to work on?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Or—maybe not “looking” exactly, because it feels more like the next piece keeps presenting itself, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with this project, it started as one idea, and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, reaching out to community people, rewriting the backend, sketching out these installation plans even though I don’t actually have the space yet… and then I’m trying to get other developers interested too, because if they could just see the structure of it—\n\nSorry. I’ll sit down and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, because there’s this project I’m trying to get off the ground, and then I’m pitching it to community leaders, and then I’m back rewriting the backend at 2 a.m. because the whole architecture needs to support the installations properly.\n\nAnd I know that sounds like I’m not answering the question, but it’s connected—I don’t just fidget with my hands, I start mock-planning whole exhibits and messaging other developers to help me, all hours, because it feels… bigger than my regular job now.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties you're experiencing causing problems in your personal relationships or daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely causing problems. It’s not just one task anymore, it’s like all these connected pieces.\n\nAnd I know I still have my regular job—or had, I mean, that’s complicated—but this project just feels bigger than that right now, so I keep adding things because they all feel necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things because you genuinely believe they need to be done, or because you just can't help doing them?\n\nPatient: I think… both, maybe? I genuinely believe it needs to be done. Like, I’m not just clicking around because I’m bored. The curator emails, the community leader pitches, rewriting the backend, planning how the installation would work, trying to get other developers involved—even if it’s late—it all feels connected.\n\nBut then, yeah, sometimes it also feels like I can’t not do it. Like if I pause, the whole thing loses momentum. And my regular job feels… smaller than this right now, honestly. This project feels bigger, like it has to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: I mean… I thought I was thinking it through at the time. It didn’t feel impulsive, it felt like timing. Like, if you wait too long, people move on, the whole window closes.\n\nBut yeah, looking back, maybe I skipped steps. I spent money we hadn’t really agreed on—GPUs, some print materials, a promo video thing, which I still think could’ve been useful because people don’t read long proposals anymore, they need to see it—but my partner was upset because we hadn’t talked it through.\n\nAnd I pushed an update without getting approval first. Not because I was trying to be reckless, it just felt like another review cycle would kill the opportunity. Like everything would stall out right when it needed to move.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your behavior has led to any significant problems in your relationships or at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it has. I don’t love saying that, because it makes it sound like everything I was doing was just wrong, and I don’t think it was. But my employer put me on leave after I pushed those changes without going through approval. They said I bypassed the process and it created problems.\n\nAnd at home it’s been… tense. My wife keeps saying the spending and the arguments are making things feel unstable. I think she means not just money, but the whole atmosphere, like she doesn’t know what I’m going to decide next. I don’t want it to be that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas because you have a clear goal in mind or because you just can't resist the idea itself?\n\nPatient: I mean, there is a clear goal. It’s not just the idea buzzing in my head, you know? I’m trying to make the project real—like actually get it in front of people. That’s why I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out how the installation would work, trying to pull in other developers.\n\nBut… it’s hard to separate that from not being able to let it go. Because once I see the path, I keep thinking, okay, this person needs to hear about it, this part needs to be rebuilt, this could connect to that group. And then suddenly it’s two in the morning and I’m still sending messages.\n\nMy regular job just feels kind of… narrow compared to it right now. This feels bigger, like it matters more. So yes, I have a goal, but the idea also sort of grabs me and keeps pulling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're drawn to new ideas or projects because they're exciting or thrilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exciting is part of it, I guess. It’s not like I’m chasing a thrill exactly, but when the project starts connecting in my head, it feels alive. Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to miss it.\n\nSo I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out installation plans—well, mock-planning them, I don’t have the space yet—and trying to get other developers interested. And it happens at weird hours because that’s when the pieces line up for me.\n\nIt feels bigger than my regular job right now. Not just more fun, but more important somehow. Like the regular work is just tickets and meetings, and this has… I don’t know, reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing you a lot of stress or distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it distress exactly. Stress, maybe, but it’s hard to separate from just having a lot happening.\n\nLike, when I’m in it, it feels productive, not stressful—more like I’m finally following the thread. But then people around me react like I’m creating a storm, and then I start feeling stressed about their reactions, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd, sorry, I’m probably going sideways here, but it reminds me of when you’re debugging something and everyone’s saying “stop touching it,” but you can see the fix is right there. So… yeah, maybe some stress. I don’t know how much is the behavior itself versus everyone trying to slow me down.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost any friendships or strained relationships with family members due to your behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah… strained, definitely. I don’t know if I’d say “lost friendships,” exactly, but things at home are not good.\n\nMy wife says the spending and the arguments have made everything feel unstable, like she doesn’t know what I’m going to do next. And I get defensive because in my head I’m thinking, well, you have to invest in something if you want it to work, right? Like you can’t build a real thing with no tools, no outreach, no—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, it’s strained things. And work too, actually. My employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes myself. I thought I was fixing something, moving it forward, but they didn’t see it that way. So… yeah, relationships and trust, both at home and at work, have taken a hit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're often looking for the next new or exciting thing to work on?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Yes. I do keep moving toward the next thing, especially when it feels connected to the bigger picture. And lately that’s been happening at all hours, because this feels bigger than my regular job now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're chasing new ideas because they're novel or exciting?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, yeah. It’s not just “new” like I’m bored and need a shiny object, though. It feels like each idea is… connected, like it’s the next necessary step.\n\nI’ll be working on one part and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, then I’m pitching community leaders, then I’m rewriting the backend because obviously the old structure won’t support it, and then I’m sketching out installation plans even though I don’t even have a space confirmed yet.\n\nAnd I keep trying to pull other developers in, because if they could just see it the way I see it, it would move faster. So yes, I guess I’m chasing it, but it feels bigger than excitement. It feels like the project is bigger than my regular job now, and I keep working on it at all hours because stopping feels like losing the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas because you have a clear plan in mind or because you're acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: I want to say it’s a clear plan, because in my head it is. And at work, I pushed an update without approval because another review cycle felt like… like letting the whole opportunity die on the vine.\n\nSo I don’t know. It felt planned to me, but I guess I was acting before anyone else had really agreed to the plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're drawn to these new projects because they offer a sense of novelty?\n\nPatient: Yeah, novelty is part of it, but it’s more like everything grabs me before I’m done with the last thing.\n\nLike I’ll be fixing a rendering bug, and then a Slack ping comes in and suddenly I’m thinking about how that message connects to the launch plan, or I’ll notice a color pattern in something and it turns into a whole visual direction. More like I felt I needed to. I mean, it was exciting too, sure, but it didn’t feel like “fun exciting,” it felt like if I didn’t do it right then, something important was going to slip away.

So yes, I have a goal, but the idea also sort of grabs me and keeps pulling.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're drawn to new ideas or projects because they're exciting or thrilling?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exciting is part of it, I guess. It’s not like I’m chasing a thrill exactly, but when the project starts connecting in my head, it feels alive. Like there’s momentum and I don’t want to miss it.\n\nSo I’ve been emailing curators, pitching community leaders, rewriting the backend, sketching out installation plans—well, mock-planning them, I don’t have the space yet—and trying to get other developers interested. Like the regular work is just tickets and meetings, and this has… I don’t know, reach.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your behavior is causing you a lot of stress or distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it distress exactly. I don’t know if I’d say “lost friendships,” exactly, but things at home are not good.\n\nMy wife says the spending and the arguments have made everything feel unstable, like she doesn’t know what I’m going to do next. And I get defensive because in my head I’m thinking, well, you have to invest in something if you want it to work, right? Like you can’t build a real thing with no tools, no outreach, no—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked.\n\nBut yes, it’s strained things. And work too, actually. My employer put me on leave after I bypassed the normal approval process and deployed changes myself. I thought I was fixing something, moving it forward, but they didn’t see it that way. So… yeah, relationships and trust, both at home and at work, have taken a hit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're often looking for the next new or exciting thing to work on?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Or—maybe not “looking” exactly, because it feels more like the next piece keeps presenting itself, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with this project, it started as one idea, and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, reaching out to community people, rewriting the backend, sketching out these installation plans even though I don’t actually have the space yet… and then I’m trying to get other developers interested too, because if they could just see the structure of it—\n\nSorry. Yes. I do keep moving toward the next thing, especially when it feels connected to the bigger picture. And lately that’s been happening at all hours, because this feels bigger than my regular job now.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're chasing new ideas because they're novel or exciting?\n\nPatient: Maybe partly, yeah. It’s not just “new” like I’m bored and need a shiny object, though. It feels like each idea is… connected, like it’s the next necessary step.\n\nI’ll be working on one part and then suddenly I’m emailing curators, then I’m pitching community leaders, then I’m rewriting the backend because obviously the old structure won’t support it, and then I’m sketching out installation plans even though I don’t even have a space confirmed yet.\n\nAnd I keep trying to pull other developers in, because if they could just see it the way I see it, it would move faster. So yes, I guess I’m chasing it, but it feels bigger than excitement. It feels like the project is bigger than my regular job now, and I keep working on it at all hours because stopping feels like losing the thread.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're acting on these ideas because you have a clear plan in mind or because you're acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: I want to say it’s a clear plan, because in my head it is. Like, there’s a sequence — hardware, outreach, visuals, deployment — it all connects.\n\nBut if I’m being honest, some of it probably looked impulsive from the outside. I spent money we hadn’t agreed on, on GPUs and print materials and these promo videos, because I felt like if I waited, the moment would pass. And at work, I pushed an update without approval because another review cycle felt like… like letting the whole opportunity die on the vine.\n\nSo I don’t know. It felt planned to me, but I guess I was acting before anyone else had really agreed to the plan.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're drawn to these new projects because they offer a sense of novelty?\n\nPatient: Yeah, novelty is part of it, but it’s more like everything grabs me before I’m done with the last thing.\n\nLike I’ll be fixing a rendering bug, and then a Slack ping comes in and suddenly I’m thinking about how that message connects to the launch plan, or I’ll notice a color pattern in something and it turns into a whole visual direction. But in the moment it felt like I was protecting the project, not just doing random things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these things because they're exciting or because you feel like you need to?\n\nPatient: More like I felt I needed to.
